=== README head ===
# Assignment 1 — Evaluation and flexibility

**IDS 575: Machine Learning and Statistical Methods for Business Analytics · Fall 2026**
Released Thursday, September 3 · **Due Thursday, October 1, 6:00 PM Central** · 10% of course grade

Covers Handouts 1–3: supervised learning and loss, training versus test error, cross-validation, choosing a split that matches deployment, logistic regression, model flexibility, bias and variance, and regularization.

**Expected effort: 6–8 focused hours.** Part A is the larger of the two parts.

Two independent cases, one memo.

- **Part A — Northline Fitness.** Build an evaluation for a churn model that reflects how the model would actually be used, and turn its predictions into a targeting decision priced in dollars. Uses Handouts 1–2.
- **Part B — Cascade Outdoor.** Measure how much of the forecast error in two retail divisions is unavoidable, compare four models against that floor, and say what a company-wide modelling standard should specify. Uses Handout 3.

Part A can be started the day the assignment is released. Part B needs Handout 3, covered in Lecture 4; you will have at least two weeks after that. Midterm 1 on September 24 covers Lectures 1–4, so this assignment is direct preparation for it, and the deadline falls a week after the exam.

---

# Part A — Northline Fitness

## The situation

Northline Fitness sells a subscription fitness app. Members pay monthly on one of three plans. About 9% of active members cancel in any 60-day window.

The retention team wants to run a monthly program: score every active member for the probability they cancel within 60 days, and send the highest-scoring members a **retention offer** — one month at 50% off plus a personal check-in.

Finance has priced it:

| Quantity | Value |
|---|---|
| Cost of extending one offer | **$18** |
| Lifetime value of a member who stays | **$240** |
| Fraction of would-be churners retained by the offer | **0.35** |
| Effect on a member who was not going to churn | none; the $18 is still spent |

So an offer to a member who would have churned is worth `240 × 0.35 - 18 = $66`. An offer to a member who would not have churned costs `$18`. No offer is worth `$0`.

A junior analyst has already pulled 24 months of history and fitted a first model: logistic regression on all available columns, 5-fold cross-validation, encoders and scalers fitted inside the folds. It reports **0.967 AUC**. The code is in `part1_northline/starter/analyst_model.py` and runs as-is.

The VP of Membership wants a second opinion before this goes to the operating committee. **Your job is to produce the estimate she can plan against, and a recommendation.**

## How the model would be deployed

If approved, the program runs monthly from **April 2026**, scoring the active book at the start of each month. Two things about that book differ from the history you have:

1. **Corporate members.** Northline signed three employer wellness contracts starting February 2025. The `corporate` channel is 0% of the book in 2024, about 20% by December 2025, and Membership Analytics forecasts **roughly 35% of the April–June 2026 book**.
2. **A competitor** launched a rival app in July 2025 and is still spending heavily.

## What you need to know about the data

All of this is also in `part1_northline/data/DATA_DICTIONARY.md`. None of it is hidden; the work is in handling it correctly, not in finding it.

- **Grain.** One row per member per calendar month. A member appears in many rows. The 4,200 members produce 33,340 rows.
- **Timing.** The dictionary states, for every column, the window over which it is measured. Some columns are recorded **after** the month being scored, and so would not exist at the moment the April 2026 model has to produce a score. Use the timing notes to decide which columns are admissible; keeping a column is a decision you should be able to justify just as much as dropping one.
- **Duplicated rows (ticket NLF-2231).** The March 2025 load was re-run after a partition failure and appended instead of replacing. Every affected row appears twice, identical on every column. Remove the duplicates before modelling.
- **Date formats (ticket NLF-2198).** `snapshot_month` appears in three formats. `load.py` provides `month_key()` to normalise them.

## A1 — Baselines (before any modelling)

Report, in one small table:

## Deliverables

Full format in `../00_start_here/04_submission_checklist.md`. One submission, one grade.

1. **`memo.pdf`** (or `.md`) — two sections in one document, each leading with its recommendation:
   - **§1 to the VP of Membership, Northline Fitness** — max 2 pages of prose, plus Exhibits A1–A3.
   - **§2 to the VP of Merchandising Planning, Cascade Outdoor** — max 1 page of prose, plus Exhibits B1–B3. Answer the three Part B questions, and say what a company-wide standard should fix and what it should leave to the data, in terms someone could apply to a third division acquired next year.

   Exhibits do not count against the prose limits.
2. **`analysis/`** — your code, runnable end to end with one command, plus a `README` saying what to run. `analysis/part1/` and `analysis/part2/` is fine; one command must still produce everything.
3. **`ai_process_note.md`** — max 1 page, covering both parts: what you delegated, what you changed or rejected and why, and how you checked the numbers.
4. **`sessions/`** — complete exported history of every AI session used.

---

| The one-standard-error rule | Handout 1, §6.5 |
| Leakage and the test that catches it | Handout 1, §7.2 |
| Splits that match deployment; grouped and temporal folds; reporting by segment | Handout 1, §7.3–7.4 |
| Logistic regression; reading a coefficient as an odds ratio | Handout 2, §7.2 |
| The flexibility dial; why the cross-validated minimum is interior | Handout 3, §1 and §4 |
| Irreducible error as a floor | Handout 3, §3.3 |
| The bias–variance decomposition | Handout 3, §3 |
| Ridge; what a penalty buys and costs; why you standardise first | Handout 3, §5.2 |

**Metrics — accuracy, precision, recall, ROC, AUC, R², RMSE, calibration — are in Handout R1, *Reading a Metric*.** Look a metric up when you need it rather than reading it front to back. The parts this assignment uses:

| For | Read |
|---|---|
| Accuracy, and why a 9% base rate makes it a poor headline | Handout R1, §4.2 |
| Confusion matrix; precision and recall | Handout R1, §4.1, §4.3 |
| ROC and AUC | Handout R1, §4.5 |
| Turning a probability into a send / don't-send decision from costs | Handout R1, §3 — the general form; A3 asks you to derive it |
| R², RMSE, MAE; error measured against a floor | Handout R1, §2 |

## Ground rules

- **Score each test set once.** Northline's test period and Cascade's replicate extract are used at the end and select nothing.
- **State every Part B error against the floor.** A raw MSE has no interpretation on its own.
- **Every number in the memo must be reproducible** from your submitted code, with one command.

## Two practical habits

**Do not let your agent read a CSV into the conversation.** `member_months.csv` is 33,000 rows; pasting it can cost more in one message than a whole working session should. Have the agent write code that reads the file and reports numbers back. The data dictionaries are short and are meant to be read.

**Start a fresh session for each work block** — the audit table, then the threshold, then Part B — rather than one session running all month. It keeps costs down and makes your transcripts easier to export and read. `../00_start_here/05_setting_up_your_agent.md` has the rest.

- **Git is clean** on `main`, three commits, nothing uncommitted. Deliverable folders [analysis/](analysis/) and [sessions/claude-code/](sessions/claude-code/) exist with `.gitkeep` placeholders.

## Blocker: no Python packages installed

The only Python on this machine is 3.11.9 at `AppData\Local\Programs\Python\Python311`, and `pip list` shows only `pip` and `setuptools`. No numpy, pandas, scikit-learn, or matplotlib. Nothing in `analysis/` or the starter scripts will run until that is fixed. Version 3.11 itself is fine (the brief wants 3.10+).

The clean fix is a virtual environment inside the folder, which `.gitignore` already excludes:

```
python -m venv .venv
.venv\Scripts\activate
pip install numpy pandas scikit-learn matplotlib
```

Want me to run that? It takes a couple of minutes and touches nothing tracked.

## Gaps to close, not urgent today

1. **`NOTES.md` does not exist.** Your CLAUDE.md says to read it at the start of every session and append at the end, for continuity across your two machines. I'll create it at the end of this session with whatever we produce.
2. **`analysis/README.md` is missing.** It is graded under criterion S: the one command, what it produces, roughly how long it takes. Worth writing as soon as the first script exists rather than at the end.
3. **`ai_process_note.md` and the memo do not exist yet.** Expected at this stage, just noting they are on the list.
4. **The repo has a GitHub remote**, `orcunTasdemir/a1_evaluation_and_flexibility`. I could not check its visibility (`gh` is not installed here). If it is public, it publishes the course's data files and brief, and later your solution, which is worth avoiding on academic-integrity grounds. Please confirm it is private on github.com.

Two nits, low stakes:

- [.gitignore](.gitignore) is the stock Python template, which ignores any directory named `lib/`, `var/`, `build/`, or `target/` at any depth. If you ever name an `analysis/lib/` helper folder, it would silently not be committed, and the submission would not run on a clean copy.
- [part2_cascade/starter/sweep.py](part2_cascade/starter/sweep.py) and [stability.py](part2_cascade/starter/stability.py) write to `sweep_outputs/` and `b3_outputs/` next to themselves, not to `analysis/outputs/`. Your own rule sends figures and saved numbers to `analysis/outputs/`, so your Part B code will want to override those paths.

## On the VS Code workspace question

**No, this is not a workspace, and you do not need one.** You have a single folder open. The [.vscode/settings.json](.vscode/settings.json) file here is folder-level configuration (it just sets the Python environment manager), which is a different thing from a workspace.

A VS Code *workspace* proper means a `.code-workspace` file: a small JSON file listing one or more root folders plus settings that apply across them. Its purpose is multi-root, letting you open, say, a frontend repo and a backend repo in one window, sharing search, settings, and a single terminal. You have no `.code-workspace` file anywhere on your Desktop or home directory, so every window you open is a plain single-folder window.

For this assignment, single-folder is the better setup. Policy §3 restricts the work to this folder, and a multi-root workspace would put other course folders in the same window where search, the terminal, and I could all reach them. The one case where a workspace would help is if you wanted the course repo's handouts open beside this folder for reference. Since the handouts are not in this folder and the README points to them in the course repo, you may want them nearby, but I would open them in a separate window rather than joining the roots.

What is the topic for this work block?

Written for: you, wrapping up setup.

Environment is built and verified. Here is where each of your six points landed.

## 1. Python environment, both machines

Created `.venv` in the project root and installed into it. Pinned versions are now in [requirements.txt](requirements.txt).

```
numpy 2.4.6 · scipy 1.17.1 · pandas 3.0.5 · scikit-learn 1.9.1 · matplotlib 3.11.2
```

On the Mac, install Python 3.11 first (`brew install python@3.11`, or the installer from python.org), then:

```
python3.11 -m venv .venv
source .venv/bin/activate
pip install -r requirements.txt
```

Three things that matter for the two-machine setup:

- **`.venv` itself never travels.** It contains Windows `.exe` files and absolute paths. It is git-ignored, and it must stay that way. `requirements.txt` is the thing that travels, and it is tracked.
- **Versions are pinned on purpose.** scikit-learn and numpy can shift numbers slightly between releases. Pinning means an AUC computed on the Windows machine reproduces on the Mac. If you ever change a version, change it in `requirements.txt` and re-run everything.
- **Match the Python minor version too.** 3.11 on both. The brief only requires 3.10+, but mixing 3.11 and 3.13 is a needless source of drift.

I smoke-tested the starters under the new environment rather than assuming they work, since pandas 3.0 is a major release. All clean: `load_raw()` returns 33,340 rows and 18 columns, `month_key()` normalises, `load_h()` returns 3,350 rows which `dedup_h()` reduces to 3,000, `load_a()` 197, `replicates.csv` 900, `make_folds()` gives 5 folds, `ALPHA_GRID` has 25 values, `QUERY_CONFIGS` has H and A, `poly_pipeline()` builds `poly → scale → est`, and `bootstrap_indices()` yields 20 draws. No data contents were read into this conversation, only shapes.

## 2. .gitignore

Edited. The stock Python template ignored `lib/`, `var/`, `build/`, `dist/`, `target/`, `instance/`, `cover/` and similar at **any** depth, so an `analysis/part2/lib/` would have vanished silently from the submission. Those patterns are now anchored to the repo root (`/lib/`, `/var/`, and so on), which is what the template intends anyway. I also added an exception so run logs saved under `analysis/outputs/` are committable, since `*.log` was being ignored.

Uncommitted right now: the `.gitignore` edit and the new `requirements.txt`. Say the word and I will commit them.

## 3. Output folder structure

Agreed, we follow the starter code. So [sweep.py](part2_cascade/starter/sweep.py) keeps writing to `sweep_outputs/` and [stability.py](part2_cascade/starter/stability.py) to `b3_outputs/`, both resolved relative to the script's own location. Since our Part B code will live in `analysis/part2/`, those same names resolve to `analysis/part2/sweep_outputs/` and `analysis/part2/b3_outputs/` with no path edits at all, which keeps the starter's structure and still puts everything under `analysis/` where the deliverable expects it. I will confirm this when we get to Part B rather than assume it now.

## 4. GitHub

Nothing to install. Git 2.52 is on PATH, Git Credential Manager is configured, and `git ls-remote` reaches the private repo, with `origin/main` matching your local HEAD. Plain `git` covers commits, pushes, and pulls, which is all this assignment needs. `gh` is only for pull requests and issues, so skip it. GitHub Desktop is good for eyeballing diffs before you push from either machine.

The habit that matters for alternating machines: `git pull` at the start of every session, `git push` at the end. `NOTES.md` is useless if it is sitting on the other laptop.

| File | What it is | Used by |

| `handoutR1_reading_a_metric.pdf` | Handout R1, *Reading a Metric*: accuracy, precision, recall, ROC, AUC, R², RMSE, calibration | A1, A3, B1 |

Put them in a `handouts/` folder at the root. My suggestion is to add `handouts/` to `.gitignore` so they stay local. They are the instructor's own material, the graders have them, and there is no reason to ship copies in your submission. Tell me which way you want it and I will set it up.

[tool_result]
# Exporting your AI session history

**IDS 575: Machine Learning and Statistical Methods for Business Analytics · Fall 2026**

Every assignment requires the complete exported history of every AI session you used. This sheet
explains how, for both approved agents.

> **Export at the end of each working session, not at the end of the assignment.** Both tools have
> some way of losing history — a retention window, a clipboard that chokes on a long conversation.
> If you wait until the night before the deadline, some of what you need may no longer exist.

> **Before you submit, open your export and read it.** Agent transcripts embed the full contents of
> files the agent read and the full output of commands it ran. Work in a clean folder that contains
> only course material.

---

## What goes in `sessions/`

```
sessions/
  README.md              which agent, how many sessions, anything the export omits
  codex/                 one file per session
  claude-code/           one file per session
```

You will normally have only one of those two subfolders. `sessions/README.md` is required either way
and can be three lines.

---

## 1. Codex

### The app

Open the session, use **Copy as Markdown** from the conversation menu, and paste into a file:

```
sessions/codex/a1-session-1.md
```

That is the whole procedure. Name the files in the order you worked; the names do not matter beyond
being distinguishable.

Two cautions:

- **It goes to the clipboard, not to a file.** Paste it somewhere immediately. A plain text editor is
  safer than Word, which has been reported to struggle with very long conversations.
- **Do it per session, while the session is open.** There is no bulk export.

### The CLI

Inside a session, `/copy` puts the conversation on the clipboard; paste it into a `.md` file the same
way.

### The transcript on disk — your backup

Codex also writes a full rollout file per session:

```
~/.codex/sessions/YYYY/MM/DD/rollout-<timestamp>-<session-uuid>.jsonl
```

Copy them if you want a belt-and-braces record:

```bash
mkdir -p sessions/codex
cp ~/.codex/sessions/2026/09/11/rollout-*.jsonl sessions/codex/
```

This is the complete log — more complete than the Markdown copy, which renders the conversation
rather than recording every tool call. **The Markdown is what I ask for. The JSONL is what saves you
if the Markdown copy failed and you did not notice.** Submitting both is fine and never penalised.

Two settings to check before you start:

- If `~/.codex/config.toml` contains `[history] persistence = "none"`, your history is being
  suppressed. Set it back to `"save-all"` (the default).
- Do not run assignment work with `--ephemeral`, which skips session persistence.

**Check the size before you attach.** Rollout files can get large, because they store repeated
context snapshots and raw tool output:

```bash
du -h sessions/codex/*
```

If the folder is over about 50 MB, zip it and say so in `sessions/README.md`.

---

## 2. Claude Code

### Option A — the raw transcript

Transcripts are stored as JSONL at:

```
~/.claude/projects/<encoded-project-path>/<session-id>.jsonl
```

`<encoded-project-path>` is your working directory with every non-alphanumeric character replaced by
`-`. Run `/status` inside a session to get the session ID; that ID is the filename. To see every
session you have, `claude --resume` opens an interactive picker.

### Option B — the readable export

From inside a session: `/export sessions/claude-code/a1-session-1.txt`

### Retention

Claude Code deletes transcripts after **30 days** by default. Copy the file into your assignment
folder at the end of each session rather than relying on the retention setting.

---

## `sessions/README.md` — templates

Ordinary case:

```markdown
# AI sessions for Assignment 1

Agent used: Codex app (4 sessions)

- codex/ : 4 Markdown exports, complete. Rollout JSONL for the same four sessions
           also included.
```

With a gap:

```markdown
# AI sessions for Assignment 1

Agent used: Claude Code (5 sessions)

- claude-code/ : 4 JSONL transcripts, complete. A fifth session on 18 September
                 was lost before I exported it; it was about half an hour, spent
                 debugging the grouped-fold split, and its result is in run_a2.py.
```

---

## If a command here does not match what you see

Both tools change quickly. If a path or a menu item on this sheet is not where it says it is,
**do not skip the export** — find the equivalent, do it, and say in `sessions/README.md` what you did
instead. Reporting that a documented step does not work is helpful and will not cost you marks.

block.get(\"type\")\n    if kind == \"text\":\n        return [block.get(\"text\", \"\")]\n    if kind == \"thinking\":\n        return [\"[thinking]\", block.get(\"thinking\", \"\")]\n    if kind == \"tool_result\":\n        content = block.get(\"content\", \"\")\n        if isinstance(content, list):\n            parts = []\n            for item in content:\n                if isinstance(item,          content = \"\\n\".join(parts)\n        return [\"[tool_result]\", str(content)]\n    return [\"[%s]\" % kind, json.dumps(block, ensure_ascii=False)]\n\n\ndef render_message(record):\n    \"\"\"One JSONL record -> list of text lines, or [] to skip.\"\"\"\n    role = record.get(\"type\")\n    if role not in (\"user\", \"assistant\", \"system\"):\n        return []\n    message = record.get(\"message\") or {}\n    content = message.get(\"content\", record.get(\"content\", \"\"))\n    lines = []\n    if isinstance(content, str):\n        lines.append(content)\n    elif isinstance(content, list):\n        for block in content:\n            if isinstance(block, dict):\n                lines.extend(render_block(block))\n            else:\n                lines.append(str(block))\n    if not any(line.strip() for line in lines):\n        return []\n    header = \"%s  [%s]\" % (role.upper(), record.get(\"timestamp\", \"no timestamp\"))\n    return [RULE, record = json.loads(line)\n            except json.JSONDecodeError:\n                skipped += 1\n                continue\n            rendered = render_message(record)\n            if rendered:\n ### What was done

- Audited the folder against the brief's "What is in this folder" list. All nine
  supplied files present, nothing missing.
- Built the Python environment. `.venv` at the repo root, pinned versions in
  `requirements.txt`. Nothing was installed before this; the system Python had
  only pip and setuptools.
- Fixed `.gitignore`. The stock Python template ignored `lib/`, `var/`, `build/`,
  `dist/`, `target/`, `instance/`, `cover/`, `htmlcov/` at any depth, so an
  `analysis/part2/lib/` would have been dropped from the submission silently.

  `analysis/outputs/` can be committed.

  `part1_northline` and `part2_cascade` is empty. The starter `OUT` paths are as
  shipped. `__pycache__` folders created by the smoke test were deleted.

### Environment

Python 3.11.9 on this machine. Recreate on the Mac with Python 3.11:

    python3.11 -m venv .venv
    source .venv/bin/activate
    pip install -r requirements.txt

`.venv` is git-ignored and does not travel. `requirements.txt` does.
Versions are pinned so numbers reproduce across the two machines.

### Design decisions made (mine)

- Clean = `month_key()` normalise, then `drop_duplicates()` on all columns.
  No cleaned CSV is written; cleaning is in code, logged to `a1_cleaning.txt`.
- Base rate reported row-level (headline, matches analyst's unit) and
  member-level (context).
- Single-variable AUCs for four columns, directions fixed from the dictionary
  before computing: days_since_last_login (+), tenure_months (−),
  app_opens_last_30d (−), avg_session_minutes (−).
- Keep the brief's two-month gap even though the label behaves like a 30-day
  window (see below): the brief fixes the windows, and the protocol should be
  safe under the documented definition.

### Findings not in the brief

- **Label structure.** Every churner has exactly one flagged row and it is
  always their last row (2,939 of 2,939). A literal 60-day window would flag
  two. In practice `churn_next_60d = 1` means "cancellation month." Evidence:
  `exit_survey_started` is 1 only on flagged rows.
- **Leakage mechanism is concrete.** The row is scored at the start of the
  month; `exit_survey_started` and `winback_offer_sent` record events during
  or after it. On 1 April 2026 both will be 0 for everyone.

=== README A1 section ===
## A1 — Baselines (before any modelling)

Report, in one small table:

1. The base rate of `churn_next_60d`.
2. The accuracy of predicting "no churn" for everyone, and one sentence on whether accuracy is the right metric for this decision.
3. AUC for one single-variable rule — for example, ranking members by `days_since_last_login`.
4. The analyst's headline AUC (0.967) beside them.

Then, in one short paragraph: what would have to be true about this dataset for 0.967 to be a good estimate of performance on the April 2026 book?


=== rubric A1 ===
11:| A1 | **Baselines.** Base rate, accuracy of the trivial rule with a correct statement of whether accuracy suits this decision, at least one single-variable AUC, and the analyst's headline beside them. The paragraph on what would have to be true for 0.967 to be credible. | **8** |

=== run.sh ===
#!/usr/bin/env bash
# One command for the whole analysis.  Run from anywhere:
#
#     bash analysis/run.sh
#
# Everything lands in analysis/outputs/.  Stdout is also saved there as
# run.log so every number in the memo has a printed source.
set -euo pipefail

HERE="$(cd "$(dirname "${BASH_SOURCE[0]}")" && pwd)"
ROOT="$(cd "$HERE/.." && pwd)"
OUT="$HERE/outputs"
mkdir -p "$OUT"

# Use the project .venv if it exists, otherwise whatever python3 is on PATH.
if [ -x "$ROOT/.venv/bin/python" ]; then PY="$ROOT/.venv/bin/python"
elif [ -x "$ROOT/.venv/Scripts/python.exe" ]; then PY="$ROOT/.venv/Scripts/python.exe"
else PY="python3"; fi

{
  echo "=== environment ==="
  "$PY" --version
  "$PY" -c "import numpy, pandas, sklearn, matplotlib; print('numpy', numpy.__version__, '| pandas', pandas.__version__, '| sklearn', sklearn.__version__, '| matplotlib', matplotlib.__version__)"
  echo

  # The analyst's pipeline, run exactly as supplied, from its own folder.  It
  # prints the headline the brief quotes; its output is kept so A1 can put
  # that number beside the baselines without anyone retyping it.
  echo "=== part1: analyst_model.py (as supplied) ==="
  "$PY" "$ROOT/part1_northline/starter/analyst_model.py" | tee "$OUT/analyst_model_output.txt"
  echo

  echo "=== part1: A1 baselines ==="
  "$PY" "$HERE/part1/a1_baselines.py"
  echo
} 2>&1 | tee "$OUT/run.log"

=== analysis/README.md ===
# Analysis — how to run

One command, from the assignment folder root:

    bash analysis/run.sh

Requires Python 3.10+ with numpy, pandas, scikit-learn and matplotlib. No
other packages. No GPU. Pinned versions used to produce the submitted numbers
are in `../requirements.txt` (Python 3.11; numpy 2.4.6, pandas 3.0.5,
scikit-learn 1.9.1, matplotlib 3.11.2). The script prefers `../.venv` if one
exists and otherwise uses `python3` on PATH.

Runtime: about 15 seconds so far (A1 only). Will be updated as parts are added.

## What it runs, in order

| Step | Script | Writes to `outputs/` |
|---|---|---|
| Analyst's pipeline, as supplied | `part1_northline/starter/analyst_model.py` | `analyst_model_output.txt` |
| A1 baselines | `part1/a1_baselines.py` | `a1_baselines.csv` (the exhibit), `a1_cleaning.txt`, and diagnostics `a1_base_rate_by_year.csv`, `a1_base_rate_by_month.csv`, `a1_column_timing.csv` |

Everything printed is also captured in `outputs/run.log`.

## Notes

- The supplied `starter/` modules are imported, not copied or modified.
- A1 has no randomness (single-variable rules are not fitted). Seeds for later
  parts are set in the scripts that need them and noted here when added.
- Data files are read in place from `part1_northline/data/` and
  `part2_cascade/data/`; nothing is written there.

[tool_result]
"""
A1 -- baselines before any modelling.  Northline Fitness churn.

What this produces, all written to analysis/outputs/:

    a1_baselines.csv            the A1 exhibit: base rate, trivial-rule
                                accuracy, single-variable AUCs, analyst's AUC
    a1_base_rate_by_year.csv    diagnostic: does the base rate move over time?
    a1_base_rate_by_month.csv   same, monthly
    a1_cleaning.txt             what dedup removed, and the uniqueness check

Cleaning done here, and nothing else: normalise snapshot_month with the
supplied month_key(), then drop exact duplicate rows (ticket NLF-2231).  No
column is dropped -- that is A2 change 1, and A1 comes before it.

Single-variable rules need no fitting, so there is no cross-validation and
no randomness in this script.  The direction of each rule is fixed below
from what the column means, before the number is seen, and is not flipped
afterwards.
"""
import re
import sys
from pathlib import Path

import numpy as np
import pandas as pd
from sklearn.metrics import accuracy_score, roc_auc_score

HERE = Path(__file__).resolve().parent
ROOT = HERE.parents[1]
OUT = ROOT / "analysis" / "outputs"

# Use the supplied loader rather than reimplementing it.
sys.path.insert(0, str(ROOT / "part1_northline" / "starter"))
from load import TARGET, load_raw, month_key  # noqa: E402

ANALYST_OUTPUT = OUT / "analyst_model_output.txt"
ANALYST_QUOTED_AUC = 0.967   # the figure in the brief, used only as a label

# Single-variable rules.  sign=+1 means "higher value -> more likely to
# churn"; sign=-1 means the opposite.  Chosen from the column definitions in
# the data dictionary, not from the data.
SINGLE_VARIABLE_RULES = {
    "days_since_last_login": (+1, "longer since last login -> more likely to churn"),
    "tenure_months":         (-1, "shorter tenure -> more likely to churn"),
    "app_opens_last_30d":    (-1, "fewer app opens -> more likely to churn"),
    "avg_session_minutes":   (-1, "shorter sessions -> more likely to churn"),
}

# Two columns the dictionary's timing notes say are recorded during or after
# the month being scored.  Diagnostic only: how do they sit against the label?
POST_SCORING_COLUMNS = ["exit_survey_started", "winback_offer_sent"]


def column_timing(df: pd.DataFrame) -> pd.DataFrame:
    """For each post-scoring column: how a 1 in it lines up with the label."""
    y = df[TARGET]
    is_last = df.index.isin(df.groupby("member_id").tail(1).index)
    rows = []
    for col in POST_SCORING_COLUMNS:
        one = df[col].eq(1)
        rows.append({
            "column": col,
            "rows_with_1": int(one.sum()),
            "share_of_rows_with_1": round(one.mean(), 4),
            "churners_among_1s": int(y[one].sum()),
            "non_churners_among_1s": int((1 - y[one]).sum()),
            "p_churn_given_1": round(y[one].mean(), 4),
            "p_churn_given_0": round(y[~one].mean(), 4),
            "share_of_churners_with_1": round(y[one].sum() / y.sum(), 4),
            "ones_on_members_last_row": int((one & is_last).sum()),
            "auc_alone": round(roc_auc_score(y, df[col]), 4),
        })
    return pd.DataFrame(rows)


def clean(df: pd.DataFrame) -> tuple[pd.DataFrame, list[str]]:
    """Normalise the month, drop exact duplicates, and record what happened."""
    notes = []
    df = df.copy()
    df["month"] = df["snapshot_month"].map(month_key)
    df["year"] = df["month"].str[:4]

    before = len(df)
    dup_mask = df.duplicated(keep="first")
    dup_months = df.loc[dup_mask, "month"].value_counts().sort_index()
    df = df.loc[~dup_mask].reset_index(drop=True)
    notes.append(f"rows as delivered           {before:,}")
    notes.append(f"exact duplicate rows removed {int(dup_mask.sum()):,}")
    notes.append(f"rows after dedup             {len(df):,}")
    notes.append("duplicates by month: " +
                 ", ".join(f"{m}={n:,}" for m, n in dup_months.items()))

    # After removing exact duplicates, one row per member per month is what
    # the dictionary promises.  Check it rather than assume it.
    remaining = df.duplicated(subset=["member_id", "month"]).sum()
    notes.append(f"(member_id, month) pairs still duplicated after dedup: {remaining:,}")
    if remaining:
        raise RuntimeError("non-identical duplicate member-months remain; inspect before continuing")

    notes.append(f"unique members               {df['member_id'].nunique():,}")
    notes.append(f"months covered               {df['month'].min()} -> {df['month'].max()} "
                 f"({df['month'].nunique()} months)")

    # How the label sits within a member's history.  The dictionary defines it
    # as a 60-day forward window, which could flag more than one row per
    # churner; record what the extract actually does.
    df = df.sort_values(["member_id", "month"]).reset_index(drop=True)
    flags_per_member = df.groupby("member_id")[TARGET].sum()
    last_rows = df.groupby("member_id").tail(1)
    notes.append(f"churners (members with >=1 flagged row)      {int((flags_per_member > 0).sum()):,}")
    notes.append(f"churners with more than one flagged row      {int((flags_per_member > 1).sum()):,}")
    notes.append(f"flagged rows that are the member's last row  {int(last_rows[TARGET].sum()):,} "
                 f"of {int(df[TARGET].sum()):,}")
    return df, notes


def analyst_auc() -> float | None:
    """Read the AUC the analyst's script printed, if run.sh has captured it."""
    if not ANALYST_OUTPUT.exists():
        return None
    m = re.search(r"cross-validated AUC\s+([0-9.]+)", ANALYST_OUTPUT.read_text())
    return float(m.group(1)) if m else None


def main() -> None:
    OUT.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)
    df, notes = clean(load_raw())
    (OUT / "a1_cleaning.txt").write_text("\n".join(notes) + "\n")
    print("\n".join(notes))
    print()

    y = df[TARGET].to_numpy()
    n_rows, n_pos = len(y), int(y.sum())

    # 1. Base rate.  Row-level is the unit the analyst's model and the monthly
    #    programme use.  Member-level is context: a member who ever churns.
    base_rate_rows = y.mean()
    base_rate_members = df.groupby("member_id")[TARGET].max().mean()

    # 2. The trivial rule: predict "no churn" for everyone.
    trivial_acc = accuracy_score(y, np.zeros_like(y))

    # 3. Single-variable rules, scored on all deduped rows.  No fitting.
    rule_rows = []
    for col, (sign, meaning) in SINGLE_VARIABLE_RULES.items():
        auc = roc_auc_score(y, sign * df[col].to_numpy())
        rule_rows.append((col, sign, meaning, auc))

    # 4. The analyst's headline, as their script printed it.
    a_auc = analyst_auc()

    table = [
        {"quantity": "base rate of churn_next_60d (rows)",
         "value": round(base_rate_rows, 4),
         "n": n_rows, "positives": n_pos,
         "note": "deduped member-months; unit matches the analyst's model"},
        {"quantity": "base rate (members ever churning)",
         "value": round(base_rate_members, 4),
         "n": df["member_id"].nunique(),
         "positives": int(df.groupby("member_id")[TARGET].max().sum()),
         "note": "context only; different unit"},
        {"quantity": "accuracy of predicting 'no churn' for everyone",
         "value": round(trivial_acc, 4), "n": n_rows, "positives": n_pos,
         "note": "= 1 - base rate; no information used"},
    ]
    for col, sign, meaning, auc in rule_rows:
        table.append({"quantity": f"AUC, single-variable rule: {col}",
                      "value": round(auc, 4), "n": n_rows, "positives": n_pos,
                      "note": f"{meaning}; all deduped rows, no fitting"})
    table.append({"quantity": "analyst's 5-fold CV AUC (all columns, raw rows)",
                  "value": a_auc if a_auc is not None else "not run",
                  "n": "33,340 raw rows", "positives": "",
                  "note": f"as printed by starter/analyst_model.py; brief quotes {ANALYST_QUOTED_AUC}"})
    exhibit = pd.DataFrame(table)
    exhibit.to_csv(OUT / "a1_baselines.csv", index=False)

    # Diagnostic: base rate over time.  Not an A1 deliverable; evidence for the
    # paragraph on whether a pooled 2024-2025 number speaks to April 2026.
    df["corporate"] = df["acquisition_channel"].eq("corporate")
    agg = dict(rows=(TARGET, "size"), churners=(TARGET, "sum"),
               base_rate=(TARGET, "mean"), corporate_share=("corporate", "mean"))
    by_year = df.groupby("year").agg(**agg).reset_index()
    by_month = df.groupby("month").agg(**agg).reset_index()
    by_year.to_csv(OUT / "a1_base_rate_by_year.csv", index=False)
    by_month.to_csv(OUT / "a1_base_rate_by_month.csv", index=False)

    # Diagnostic: the two columns the dictionary dates after the scoring moment.
    timing = column_timing(df)
    timing.to_csv(OUT / "a1_column_timing.csv", index=False)

    print("A1 exhibit")
    print(exhibit.to_string(index=False))
    print()
    print("base rate by year (diagnostic)")
    print(by_year.to_string(index=False, float_format=lambda v: f"{v:.4f}"))
    print()
    print("base rate by month, last six (diagnostic)")
    print(by_month.tail(6).to_string(index=False, float_format=lambda v: f"{v:.4f}"))
    print()
    print("post-scoring columns against the label (diagnostic)")
    print(timing.T.to_string(header=False))
    print()
    print(f"wrote {OUT / 'a1_baselines.csv'}, a1_base_rate_by_year.csv, "
          f"a1_base_rate_by_month.csv, a1_column_timing.csv, a1_cleaning.txt")


if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

# A1 draft — baselines

Working draft for the A1 part of memo §1 (to the VP of Membership). Not the
memo. Every number here is in `analysis/outputs/` from `bash analysis/run.sh`
(session 2, 2026-09-17) unless marked otherwise. Prose budget: §1 is two pages
for A1–A3 together, so A1 should be about a third of a page plus the exhibit.

---

## Exhibit A1 — Baselines before any modelling

Source: `analysis/outputs/a1_baselines.csv`, `a1_cleaning.txt`.

| Quantity | Value | Basis |
|---|---:|---|
| Base rate of `churn_next_60d` | **9.29%** (2,939 of 31,645 member-months) | deduplicated rows, Jan 2024 – Dec 2025 |
| *Context:* members who churn at some point in the window | 70.0% (2,939 of 4,200) | member level; a different unit, shown for scale |
| Accuracy of predicting "no churn" for everyone | **90.71%** | = 1 − base rate; uses no information |
| AUC, rank members by `avg_session_minutes` (shorter → churn) | **0.681** | one column, no fitting, all deduplicated rows |
| AUC, rank by `app_opens_last_30d` (fewer → churn) | 0.648 | " |
| AUC, rank by `days_since_last_login` (longer → churn) | 0.646 | " |
| AUC, rank by `tenure_months` (shorter → churn) | 0.502 | " |
| **Analyst's 5-fold cross-validated AUC, all 14 columns** | **0.967** | raw 33,340 rows, random shuffled folds, as printed by `analyst_model.py` |

Footnote for the exhibit: the extract contains 1,695 exact duplicate rows, all
in March 2025 (ticket NLF-2231), removed before every number above except the
analyst's, which is reported as their script prints it on the raw file.

---

## Prose (draft, ~180 words)

**Where the model would have to start from.** About one member-month in
eleven ends in a cancellation: the base rate is 9.29%, 2,939 flagged rows out of
31,645 after removing the duplicated March 2025 load. A rule that predicts "no
churn" for everyone is therefore 90.7% accurate while identifying nobody.
Accuracy is the wrong yardstick here for two reasons: the outcome is rare, so
the number is dominated by the 91% of rows where nothing happens; and the two
mistakes cost different amounts — a wasted offer costs $18, a churner not
offered forgoes $66 — so counting them equally is the wrong loss. The number
that fits the decision is the dollar value of the programme at the
cost-derived threshold (Exhibit A3); AUC is used below only to compare
evaluation protocols before any threshold exists.

Ranking members by a single behavioural column, with no model at all, gives
an AUC between 0.65 and 0.68 (shorter sessions, fewer app opens, longer since
last login). Tenure on its own carries no ranking signal (0.50). The analyst's
model reports 0.967.

**What would have to be true for 0.967 to describe April 2026.** Four
things. The data dictionary's timing notes would have to be wrong: every
column the model uses would have to be known at the start of the month rather
than filled in afterwards — but `winback_offer_sent` is written from an
at-risk list circulated after month close, and `exit_survey_started` is the
cancellation flow itself, set on 1,810 rows, every one of them a cancellation
month and none a non-churner. The April book would have to resemble the pooled 2024–25 file — but corporate
members were 0% of 2024 rows, 10% of 2025 rows, 20% of December 2025, and are
forecast at 35%. The churn process would have to be stable across the two
years — but the monthly rate ranged from 5.3% (April 2025) to 14.2% (August
2025), with the two highest months coinciding with the competitor's July 2025
launch. And predicting a member's month from that same member's other months
would have to count as prediction — which is what random folds permit. Set
against a best single-column AUC of 0.68, a jump to 0.97 from a linear
combination of fourteen columns is the pattern the course describes as a leak
until shown otherwise. Section A2 tests each of these one at a time.

---

## Notes to self (not memo text)

### What the label actually is

From `a1_cleaning.txt`: 2,939 churners, none with more than one flagged row,
and every flagged row is the member's last row. The dictionary defines the
target as a 60-day forward window, which would normally flag two rows per
churner. The extract flags one: the cancellation month itself. So in practice
`churn_next_60d = 1` means "this is the member's final month on the book."
Worth one line in the memo (shows the dictionary was checked, not trusted).
It also means the brief's two-month gap between training and validation is
conservative; keep it anyway — the brief fixes the windows for comparability,
and a protocol should be safe under the documented definition.

### Evidence for the leakage claim

Source: `analysis/outputs/a1_column_timing.csv`. Usable in the memo.

| | `exit_survey_started` | `winback_offer_sent` |
|---|---:|---:|
| Rows with a 1 | 1,810 (5.7%) | 3,533 (11.2%) |
| Churners among them | 1,810 — all | 2,239 (63.4%) |
| Non-churners among them | 0 | 1,294 |
| P(churn \| 0) | 3.8% | 2.5% |
| Share of churners with a 1 | 61.6% | 76.2% |
| 1s on the member's last row | 1,810 of 1,810 | 2,288 of 3,533 |
| AUC of the column alone | 0.808 | 0.858 |

Mechanism, in one sentence: the row is scored at the start of the month, but
these two columns record events during or after it, so on 1 April 2026 they
will be 0 for everyone — including the members who go on to cancel — and what
the model learned from them is worthless at deployment.

The framing for the memo paragraph (yours): for 0.967 to hold, the
dictionary's timing notes would have to be wrong — the columns would have to
be known at the start of the month rather than filled in afterwards. The
extract says they were filled in afterwards: a column that is 1 only on
cancellation months and never on a non-churner is recording the cancellation,
not predicting it.

### Arguing against the draft

- The 0.68 → 0.97 gap is a suspicion, not proof; fourteen columns can beat any
  one. Keep "until shown otherwise"; A2 change 1 is the test.
- Seasonality vs competitor: 2024 also peaked in Jul–Sep (12.4–13.3%). Two
  years cannot separate the two; the draft says "coinciding with," not "caused
  by." Keep it that way.
- `tenure_months` at 0.50 is not a bug: churn may be non-monotone in tenure,
  which a single ranking cannot capture — and a linear logit cannot either.
- 70% of members churning over 24 months is consistent with a 9% 60-day rate
  (a hazard of roughly 4.5%/month compounds to ~67% over 24 months).
- Row counts differ from the analyst's (31,645 vs 33,340) because of dedup;
  the duplicated March 2025 rows have a below-average rate (6.0%), so dedup
  raises the base rate slightly. Footnoted in the exhibit.
- Everything came out as expected. Course doc §4: that is the moment to slow
  down, not speed up. The one thing not expected — the label structure — was
  found by checking rather than trusting.

### Numbers used and where they live

| Number | File |
|---|---|
| 9.29%, 2,939 / 31,645; 70.0%, 2,939 / 4,200; 90.71%; AUCs 0.681 / 0.648 / 0.646 / 0.502; 0.967 | `a1_baselines.csv` |
| 1,695 duplicates, all 2025-03; 31,645 rows; one flag per churner, 2,939 of 2,939 on last row | `a1_cleaning.txt` |
| 2024: 10.61%, corporate 0%; 2025: 8.62%, corporate 10.1% | `a1_base_rate_by_year.csv` |
| 5.3% (2025-04), 14.2% (2025-08), 2024 Jul–Sep 12.4–13.3%, corporate 20.2% in 2025-12 | `a1_base_rate_by_month.csv` |
| 1,810 / 0 / 61.6% / 0.808; 3,533 / 2,239 / 76.2% / 0.858 | `a1_column_timing.csv` |

### How to use the leakage evidence in the memo (settled end of session 2)

The *reason* to drop `exit_survey_started` and `winback_offer_sent` is the
dictionary's timing notes: not available at the moment of scoring. That is
what the rubric grades ("argued from availability at the moment of scoring").
The cross-tab and single-column AUCs are *corroboration* that the timing note
holds in this extract, and they size what A2 change 1 should do to the
number. Order in the memo: timing argument first, evidence second. Never
"dropped because they predict too well" — a genuinely available column with
a high AUC should be kept.

[tool_result]
# `member_months.csv` — data dictionary

Extract prepared by the Membership Analytics team, **March 2026**.
Source systems: Billing (Zuora), Product Analytics (app event stream), Zendesk, Salesforce.

**Grain.** One row per **member per calendar month** in which the member was active at any point during that month. A member appears once for every month they were on the book. Members who cancel stop appearing after the month in which they cancelled.

**Window.** January 2024 through December 2025. All outcomes have matured: the last snapshot month is December 2025, whose 60-day outcome window closed at the end of February 2026, before this extract was pulled.

---

## Columns

| Column | Type | Source | Definition |
|---|---|---|---|
| `member_id` | int | Billing | Stable member identifier. |
| `snapshot_month` | str | Billing | The calendar month the row describes. *Note: this field was written by three different pipeline versions over the extract window and the formatting is not consistent.* |
| `signup_month` | str | Billing | Month the member's first paid subscription started. |
| `acquisition_channel` | str | Salesforce | How the member was acquired: `organic`, `paid_search`, `partner_gym`, `corporate`. The `corporate` channel covers members enrolled through an employer wellness contract; the first such contract went live in February 2025. |
| `plan_tier` | str | Billing | `basic`, `plus`, or `elite`. |
| `monthly_price` | float | Billing | List price of the plan in USD. Does not reflect promotional discounts. |
| `city_tier` | str | Salesforce | `metro`, `suburban`, `rural`. Self-reported at signup. |
| `tenure_months` | int | Billing | Completed months since `signup_month`, as of the start of `snapshot_month`. |
| `sessions_last_30d` | int | Product Analytics | Completed workout sessions logged in the 30 days ending on the **last day of the previous month**. |
| `app_opens_last_30d` | float | Product Analytics | App launches over the same 30-day window. |
| `avg_session_minutes` | float | Product Analytics | Mean session length over the same window. |
| `days_since_last_login` | float | Product Analytics | Days between the member's last app login and the **last day of the previous month**. Capped at 90. |
| `support_tickets_last_30d` | int | Zendesk | Tickets opened by the member over the same 30-day window. |
| `payment_failures_last_90d` | int | Billing | Declined or failed charge attempts in the 90 days ending on the **last day of the previous month**. A failure is recorded when the card issuer declines; it does not imply the member intended to cancel. |
| `referrals_made` | int | Product Analytics | Successful referrals, same 30-day window. |
| `winback_offer_sent` | int | Salesforce | `1` if the Retention team contacted this member about this month. **The Retention team works from the at-risk list that Membership Analytics circulates on the third business day after month close.** |
| `exit_survey_started` | int | Product Analytics | `1` if the member opened the cancellation exit survey. |
| `churn_next_60d` | int | Billing | **Target.** `1` if the member's subscription terminated within 60 days of the end of `snapshot_month`. |

---

## Known issues logged against this extract

- **NLF-2231** — The March 2025 load was re-run after a partition failure. The re-run was not idempotent. *Status: open.*
- **NLF-2198** — `snapshot_month` formatting varies by pipeline version. *Status: won't fix, downstream consumers handle it.*

> Left all columns in — figured the model can decide what's useful. Didn't do anything about NLF-2231, shouldn't matter for modelling. Corporate members are a small slice so I didn't look at them separately.

=== A2 section of brief ===
## A2 — Build an evaluation that matches deployment

Make the following four changes to the analyst's protocol, **one at a time, in an order you choose**, and record the AUC after each. Report it as a table.

Until you have made change 3 the number is cross-validated on the building data; from change 3 onward it is measured on the test window defined below. Say in the table which kind each row is.

| Change | What it means |
|---|---|
| **1. Admissible columns only** | Keep only columns that exist at the moment of scoring, per the timing notes in the dictionary. |
| **2. One member, not many rows** | A member contributes many rows. Make sure a member never appears in both the fitting data and the held-out data. |
| **3. Past to future** | Fit on earlier months and evaluate on later ones, rather than on a random shuffle of all months. |
| **4. Report by segment** | Report the final number separately for `corporate` members and for everyone else. |

**One of these four changes will move the number a great deal, and at least one will move it barely at all.** That is expected. A change can be the right thing to do and still be worth almost nothing on a particular dataset, and reporting a near-zero effect correctly earns the same marks as reporting a large one. Do not go looking for a bug because a number did not move.

For each row of the table, write one sentence on what the number means. Then, in two or three sentences:

- Why you chose that order.
- Whether the changes between rows can be read as "problem X cost this much AUC." (They cannot be read that way independently of the order; say why, in your own words.)

**Use these evaluation windows** for the final protocol, so that everyone's numbers are comparable:

| Window | Months | Purpose |
|---|---|---|
| Training | 2024-01 → 2025-06 | Fit the model |
| Not used | 2025-07, 2025-08 | Labels for June 2025 do not mature until end of August 2025 |
| Validation | 2025-09 | Any choice made by looking at data |
| Test | 2025-10 → 2025-12 | Final estimate; score once |

Explain in one sentence why the two-month gap is there.

## What A1 asked and what you produced

All numbers from `bash analysis/run.sh`, in `analysis/outputs/a1_baselines.csv` unless noted.

| Brief item | Your answer | Why it is there |
|---|---|---|
| 1. Base rate | **9.29%**, 2,939 of 31,645 deduped rows | The do-nothing answer. Everything else is measured against it. Member-level 70.0% is shown for scale only, different unit. |
| 2. Trivial-rule accuracy | **90.71%** | Equals 1 minus base rate, uses no information. The sentence: accuracy is wrong because the outcome is rare (the number is dominated by the 91% where nothing happens) and because the two mistakes cost different amounts ($18 vs $66). |
| 3. Single-variable AUC | **0.681** `avg_session_minutes`; 0.648, 0.646, 0.502 for the others | What ranking with no model at all buys. Directions fixed from the dictionary before computing, not flipped afterwards. |
| 4. Analyst's headline | **0.967**, read from `analyst_model_output.txt`, not retyped | Sits beside 0.68 so the gap is visible. |
| The paragraph | Four things would have to be true | Timing notes wrong; April book resembles pooled 2024–25; churn process stable; predicting a member from their own other months counts as prediction. A2 tests each one at a time. |

## The ideas underneath, in one line each

- **Base rate** is the prior. A model has to beat it, and a metric that is flattered by it (accuracy) cannot tell you whether the model did.
- **AUC** is the probability that a randomly chosen churner is ranked above a randomly chosen non-churner. 0.5 is a coin flip, and it does not depend on the base rate or on any threshold, which is why it is the right tool for comparing evaluation *protocols* in A2 before any threshold exists. It is not the right tool for the *decision*; that is dollars at a threshold, A3.
- **Single-variable baseline** calibrates what "good" means on this data. If one column gets 0.68, then 0.97 from a linear combination of fourteen is a claim that needs defending, not a result to celebrate. Handout 2's rule: treat a jump like that as a leak until shown otherwise.
- **"What would have to be true"** is the right shape of question because 0.967 is a real number. It is an honest estimate of *something*. The question is whether that something is April 2026.

## Three things you found that the brief did not hand you

1. **The label marks one row per churner, always the last one** (2,939 of 2,939, `a1_cleaning.txt`). A literal 60-day window would flag two or three. So in practice `churn_next_60d = 1` means "this is the member's final month," and the features on that row were measured through the end of the *previous* month. The effective horizon is about one month, not sixty days. You chose to keep the brief's two-month gap anyway. Right call: the brief fixes the windows for comparability, and the protocol should be safe under the documented definition.
2. **The whole of March 2025 is duplicated.** 1,695 duplicates removed, 1,695 rows remaining for that month. Every row appears exactly twice, which is what "appended instead of replacing" means. The draft's "6.0%" for the duplicated rows is the deduped March rate, 0.0596 in `a1_base_rate_by_month.csv`, and it is the same thing because the whole month was doubled. That also reconciles the analyst's 0.0912 with your 0.0929: (2,939 + 101) / 33,340 = 0.0912.
3. **The two post-scoring columns are recording the cancellation, not predicting it.** `exit_survey_started` is 1 on 1,810 rows, every one a churner's last row, never a non-churner. `winback_offer_sent` is set from a list circulated *after* month close. On 1 April 2026 both are 0 for everyone. The draft's ordering is right: the *reason* to drop them is the timing note, the cross-tab is corroboration.

No, and this is worth getting straight because it is the exact trap the exhibit exists to prevent.

**90.71% and 0.967 are on different scales and cannot be compared to each other.** They are two different metrics measuring two different things.

- **90.71% is accuracy.** The fraction of rows where a yes/no call was correct. The do-nothing rule says "no" to everyone and is right 90.71% of the time, because 90.71% of rows are in fact "no." It identifies zero churners.
- **0.967 is AUC.** It says nothing about yes/no calls. It measures ranking: the probability that a randomly chosen churner is scored above a randomly chosen non-churner. There is no threshold in it, so there is no "correct" or "incorrect" in it.

So the exhibit is really two separate ladders, and the reader has to be kept from reading across them.

**The accuracy ladder** has one rung: 90.71% for doing nothing. The analyst did not report accuracy, and you do not need to compute it. The rung is there to make one point: a number in the nineties can come from a rule that does nothing at all, so accuracy is not how the model will be judged. That is the sentence the rubric asks for.

**The AUC ladder** is where the comparison actually happens:

| Rung | AUC |
|---|---|
| Do nothing (score everyone the same, so no ranking at all) | 0.50 |
| Rank by one column, no model | 0.65 to 0.68 |
| Analyst's model, fourteen columns, random folds | 0.967 |

The do-nothing rule lands at 0.50 on this ladder, not 90.71%. Every row is tied, so a churner beats a non-churner exactly half the time. A coin flip.

The comparison you are making in A1 is **0.68 versus 0.967**: what one column with no fitting achieves, against what the analyst claims. The gap between them is the thing that has to be explained, and the four assumptions in your paragraph are the candidate explanations.

One presentation choice, yours to make: your exhibit currently shows the do-nothing rule on the accuracy ladder only. Adding its AUC (0.50) as a row would put the same rule on both ladders and make the "two scales" point visible in the table itself, so a VP cannot read 90.71 and 96.7 as neighbours. It is a one-line addition to the script if you want it. If you prefer to make the point in prose instead, that works too.

Does the two-ladders picture settle it? If so, question 1 in the quiz is now half answered, and the other half is why the *decision* uses neither ladder.

Draft updated: a `0.50` row under the accuracy row, one sentence in the first paragraph saying the two numbers are on different scales, and the ranking paragraph now opens "Against that 0.50". Four files changed, nothing committed yet.

Now the quiz. Verdicts first, then what is thin.

## 1. Why AUC, and what the decision uses instead

**Mostly right, one garble, one gap.** Right: AUC is threshold-free, so it is the one number you can put on every row of the A2 table before a threshold exists. The garbled part is "unless there is a unique distribution to the scores." The caveat you are reaching for is this: AUC averages ranking quality over *all* thresholds, but the program lives at *one* threshold. Two models with the same AUC can differ at t\*, and a higher AUC does not guarantee more dollars at t\*. So AUC ranks protocols, it does not price the program.

The gap is the second half. The decision uses neither ladder because it needs two things AUC does not have: a threshold derived from $18 and $66, and probabilities that are calibrated well enough that "p above t\*" means what it says. Value at t\* is 66 × (churners offered) minus 18 × (non-churners offered), and that is A3.

## 2. Why tenure scored 0.50

**Wrong inference.** "No meaningful difference in distribution" is what 0.50 would mean if AUC could detect any difference, but it cannot. AUC measures *monotone* ranking only: does higher tenure go with more churn, or with less. A column where churn is high at low tenure, high again around a renewal point, and low in between can have very different distributions for churners and non-churners and still score 0.50 as a single ranking, because the two ends cancel.

The implication for logistic regression is the payoff of the question: a linear logit is also monotone in each feature. It fits one coefficient per column, so it will also get almost nothing from tenure unless tenure is transformed, binned, or interacted. That is why the draft says "not a bug." It is a hedge right now ("may be non-monotone"); a churn-rate-by-tenure-bucket table would turn it into a fact. Say the word and I will add it as a diagnostic.

## 3. Ways 0.967 is optimistic

**You listed real things but mixed two different failures.** Sort them:

*Ways the CV number is inflated as an estimate of its own protocol:*
- (a) The two post-scoring columns.
- (b) The same member in fitting and held-out folds, so the model recognises people rather than predicts them. A2 change 2.
- (c) Random shuffle lets a fold train on later months and validate on earlier ones. A2 change 3. This is distinct from (b), and you ran them together.
- (d) The duplicates. You named them but not why: an exact copy of a validation row sitting in the training fold is memorisation, the strongest possible form of (b). It is 1,695 rows, small, and A2 will show how small.

*Ways an honest number would still not describe April 2026:*
- (e) The book has moved: corporate 0% in 2024, 35% forecast. Competitor. Season.

These are different claims. (a) to (d) say "the estimate is wrong about the data it has." (e) says "the data is not the deployment." Both go in the paragraph, and your draft already separates them; your answer did not.

One correction to how you phrased it: the dictionary's **timing notes are right**, and they are the basis of change 1. What the extract deviates from is the **label definition** (60-day window written, last-row flag delivered). If the memo says "the timing notes are a little wrong," it undercuts your own change 1 argument. Keep those two apart.

## 4. Seasonality versus competitor

**Overclaimed, and the numbers do not support it.** You said one year swung almost 10 points and the other about 1. From `a1_base_rate_by_month.csv`: 2024 ran 6.4% to 13.3%, a 6.9-point swing; 2025 ran 5.3% to 14.2%, 8.9 points. Both years peak in July to September at similar levels, 12.4 to 13.3 versus 10.8 to 14.2. Seasonality is the dominant pattern.

What 2025 does show is a bigger *step* into July, 6.7 to 12.6 versus 10.6 to 13.3 the year before. But that is mostly because spring 2025 was unusually low, 5.3 to 7.1%, and spring 2025 is also when the corporate influx started. So the low spring has a competing explanation, and the competitor's incremental effect, if any, is a couple of points that two years cannot separate from noise. Your draft says "coinciding with" and that is the correct strength. Your answer said "backed by data and very probable," and it is not.

This is exactly the habit the course is drilling: you stated "10% versus 1%" from memory, and it was wrong. The rule is not there for style.

The useful thing seasonality does give you: the test window is October to December and deployment is April to June. Those are different seasons with different base rates (Q4 2025 ran 8.4 to 9.5%, Apr to Jun 2025 ran 5.3 to 7.1%). Keep that for A3, where the dollar estimate depends on how many churners there are to find.

## 5. The leakage argument

**Good, best of the five.** The reverse-causation framing is right: the column is high-AUC because the outcome caused it, not the other way. And "if it were genuinely available, keep it" is the correct rule.

Two refinements. First, drop "ad hoc"; the word you want is *post-scoring* or *recorded after the moment of scoring*. The test is concrete: would this value exist on 1 April 2026 for the row being scored? For both columns, no. Second, `winback_offer_sent` is not purely a cancellation record. It is 1 on 1,294 *non-churners*. It is an intervention, and it is the very intervention the program proposes to make. That makes it inadmissible for the timing reason and also a confound: some of the history's outcomes are post-treatment. File that for A3.

## Where this leaves A1

Answers 1 and 5 would pass an oral check with minor tightening. Answer 2 needs the monotone-versus-any-difference distinction. Answers 3 and 4 need the separations above, and 4 needs the real numbers.

Uncommitted: the script change, its outputs, `run.log`, and the draft. Commit now, or after you have re-read the draft's new sentences?

Good that we checked, because the data says something different from what I told you.

## 2. Tenure: what the data actually shows

The mechanism I described is real: AUC only detects monotone relationships, so a column where churn is high at both ends and low in the middle can score 0.50 while still carrying information, and a linear logit would miss it for the same reason. Binning is the fix in that case.

**But that is not what happened here.** Churn rate by tenure bucket, deduped rows:

| Tenure (months) | Rows | Churn rate |
|---|---:|---:|
| 0 | 247 | 9.7% |
| 1 | 446 | 7.4% |
| 2 | 642 | 6.7% |
| 3 | 717 | 7.9% |
| 4 to 5 | 1,631 | 8.3% |
| 6 to 8 | 2,547 | 10.1% |
| 9 to 11 | 2,565 | 9.7% |
| 12 | 884 | 9.2% |
| 13 to 17 | 4,798 | 9.8% |
| 18 to 23 | 5,933 | 9.7% |
| 24+ | 11,235 | 9.0% |

It is flat. Every bucket sits between 6.7% and 10.1%, around a 9.3% base rate, with no renewal spike at month 12 and no early-tenure cliff. Tenure is near-uninformative about churn *at any range*, so binning would not rescue it either. The draft's current hedge, "churn may be non-monotone in tenure," is the wrong story for this data and should go.

The honest sentence for the draft is something like: *Tenure alone carries no ranking signal (0.50), and this is not a monotonicity artefact: the churn rate sits between 6.7% and 10.1% in every tenure bucket from month 0 to 24+.* That says the 0.50 was checked rather than shrugged at, which is the point.

If you agree, I will add the bucket table to `a1_baselines.py` as a diagnostic output (`a1_churn_by_tenure.csv`) so the numbers have a source in the run, and swap the draft sentence. The general monotone lesson is still true and still worth knowing for the midterm; it just is not the explanation here.

## 3. Corporate: you are not wrong, you were mixing two claims

Everything you said is true. Corporate goes from 0% to 35% of the book, the training data barely knows them, and a number estimated on the old book need not hold on the new one. That belongs in the paragraph, and your draft has it.

What I objected to was putting it in the same list as the leaks. They are different failures with different fixes:

- **Leaks and fold contamination** (columns, member overlap, shuffled time, duplicates) make 0.967 wrong *about the historical data itself*. Fix them and the number drops to something honest about 2024–25. That is A2 changes 1 to 3.
- **The book moving** (corporate mix, competitor, season) means even a perfectly honest 2024–25 number describes a population that April 2026 will not be. No amount of fixing the protocol changes this. The response is to measure it, which is A2 change 4, report by segment, and then say how much weight the segment number can bear.

Keep them apart in the memo because the VP needs to hear two different things: "the analyst's number was inflated" and "the honest number still may not transfer."

Two refinements while we are here. First, distribution shift does not automatically *lower* AUC. Ranking within the corporate segment could be easier or harder; you do not know until change 4 reports it. What you can say a priori is that the estimate is uncertain, not that it is too high. Second, the corporate numbers are striking, and they will matter for A2 and A3:

- Corporate churn rate in the data is **3.1%** against **9.7%** for everyone else.
- Corporate members are all young: tenure 0 to 12 months over the whole extract.
- Inside the training window (through June 2025) there are only **533 corporate rows**, maximum tenure **6 months**.

So the segment the model will mostly be scoring in April 2026 is one it has seen for at most six months of history, at a third of the base rate. That is the concrete version of "training on outdated data," and it is a number you can put in the paragraph now if you want it, or hold for A2 change 4 where it lands naturally. Your call; either way I will record it in `NOTES.md` so it is not lost.

Say yes or no on the tenure diagnostic and the draft sentence, and whether the corporate numbers go into A1 or wait for A2.

=== analyst_model.py ===
"""
The junior analyst's pipeline, exactly as they ran it.

This file is given to you so that you do not have to reverse-engineer the
headline number.  It runs, it reproduces the figures quoted in the brief, and
it is wrong.  Your job is to find out *why* it is wrong -- not to rebuild it.

Read it.  Every line is a defensible-looking choice, and several of them are
the assignment.  Note in particular that the modelling itself is careful: the
encoder and the scaler are fitted inside each fold, so there is no
preprocessing leak here.  Whatever is wrong with this number is wrong at the
level of the protocol, not the pipeline.

    python3 analyst_model.py

Expected output (Python 3.10+, scikit-learn >= 1.3; small version drift is
normal and does not matter):

    rows                       33,340
    5-fold cross-validated AUC  0.967
    5-fold cross-validated AP   0.899
    estimated programme value   $19,774 per quarter
"""
import warnings
from pathlib import Path

import numpy as np
import pandas as pd
from sklearn.compose import ColumnTransformer
from sklearn.linear_model import LogisticRegression
from sklearn.metrics import average_precision_score, roc_auc_score
from sklearn.model_selection import StratifiedKFold
from sklearn.pipeline import Pipeline
from sklearn.preprocessing import OneHotEncoder, StandardScaler

warnings.filterwarnings("ignore")

DATA = Path(__file__).resolve().parents[1] / "data" / "member_months.csv"

# Economics from the finance team, per the brief.
OFFER_COST = 18.0        # cost of extending one retention offer, USD
MEMBER_LTV = 240.0       # value of a member who stays, USD
OFFER_CONVERSION = 0.35  # fraction of would-be churners retained by the offer

TARGET = "churn_next_60d"
# The analyst dropped only the identifier and the two date strings, on the
# grounds that they are not numeric features.  Everything else was kept:
# "figured the model can decide what's useful."
NOT_FEATURES = (TARGET, "member_id", "snapshot_month", "signup_month")


def analyst_pipeline(categorical, numeric) -> Pipeline:
    """One-hot the categoricals, standardise the rest, fit logistic regression.

    Everything fitted from data lives inside this object, so when it is fitted
    on a fold's training portion the encoder and the scaler see only that
    portion.  This much the analyst got right.
    """
    return Pipeline([
        ("prep", ColumnTransformer([
            ("cat", OneHotEncoder(handle_unknown="ignore", drop="first"), categorical),
            ("num", StandardScaler(), numeric),
        ])),
        ("est", LogisticRegression(max_iter=2000)),
    ])


def main() -> None:
    df = pd.read_csv(DATA)
    y = df[TARGET].to_numpy()

    features = [c for c in df.columns if c not in NOT_FEATURES]
    categorical = [c for c in features if not pd.api.types.is_numeric_dtype(df[c])]
    numeric = [c for c in features if c not in categorical]
    X = df[features]

    folds = StratifiedKFold(n_splits=5, shuffle=True, random_state=0)
    oof = np.zeros(len(df))
    for train_idx, test_idx in folds.split(X, y):
        model = analyst_pipeline(categorical, numeric)
        model.fit(X.iloc[train_idx], y[train_idx])
        oof[test_idx] = model.predict_proba(X.iloc[test_idx])[:, 1]

    auc = roc_auc_score(y, oof)
    ap = average_precision_score(y, oof)

    # Value of one targeting decision: offer a churner and you net
    # LTV * conversion - cost; offer a non-churner and you lose the cost.
    realised = y * MEMBER_LTV * OFFER_CONVERSION - OFFER_COST
    grid = np.linspace(0.005, 0.6, 400)
    values = [float(np.sum((oof > t) * realised)) for t in grid]
    best = int(np.argmax(values))
    # 24 months of data = 8 quarters.
    per_quarter = values[best] / 8.0

    print(f"rows                        {len(df):,}")
    print(f"unique members              {df['member_id'].nunique():,}")
    print(f"features used               {len(features)}")
    print(f"base rate                   {y.mean():.4f}")
    print()
    print(f"5-fold cross-validated AUC  {auc:.3f}")
    print(f"5-fold cross-validated AP   {ap:.3f}")
    print(f"optimal threshold           {grid[best]:.3f}")
    print(f"estimated programme value   ${per_quarter:,.0f} per quarter")
    print()
    print("Recommendation: strong signal, move to production this quarter.")


if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

=== load.py ===
"""
Assignment 1 starter.  Northline Fitness churn.

This file exists so you do not have to retype boilerplate.  It deliberately
does NOT clean the data, choose features, or drop any column.  Every one of
those is a decision you are being graded on.

The evaluation windows below are the ones the brief tells you to use.  They
are here so your A3 numbers are comparable to everyone else's -- you are
graded on explaining why this shape is right, not on inventing the boundaries.

Usage:
    from load import load_raw, month_key, TRAIN_END, VALID_MONTH, TEST_START
    df = load_raw()
    df["month"] = df["snapshot_month"].map(month_key)
"""
from pathlib import Path

import pandas as pd

DATA = Path(__file__).resolve().parents[1] / "data" / "member_months.csv"

# The economics from the assignment brief.
OFFER_COST = 18.0        # cost of extending one retention offer, USD
MEMBER_LTV = 240.0       # value of a member who stays, USD
OFFER_CONVERSION = 0.35  # fraction of would-be churners retained by the offer

TARGET = "churn_next_60d"

# The evaluation windows from the brief, as 'YYYY-MM' strings.
# Training  : months <= TRAIN_END
# Not used  : 2025-07, 2025-08   (labels for the last training month mature at
#                                 the end of August 2025)
# Validation: month == VALID_MONTH
# Test      : months >= TEST_START   -- touch this once, at the end
TRAIN_END = "2025-06"
VALID_MONTH = "2025-09"
TEST_START = "2025-10"


def load_raw() -> pd.DataFrame:
    """Read the extract exactly as delivered.  No cleaning."""
    return pd.read_csv(DATA)


def month_key(value: str) -> str:
    """Normalise the three `snapshot_month` formats to 'YYYY-MM'.

    You still have to decide whether and how to use the month.  This just
    stops the three formats from silently becoming three different categories.
    """
    s = str(value).strip()
    if "/" in s:                      # 'MM/YYYY'
        month, year = s.split("/")
        return f"{year}-{int(month):02d}"
    return s[:7]                      # 'YYYY-MM' or 'YYYY-MM-DD'


def offer_value(churned: int, offered: int) -> float:
    """Realised dollar value of one targeting decision, per the brief."""
    if not offered:
        return 0.0
    return MEMBER_LTV * OFFER_CONVERSION - OFFER_COST if churned else -OFFER_COST


if __name__ == "__main__":
    df = load_raw()
    df["month"] = df["snapshot_month"].map(month_key)
    print(f"rows                {len(df):,}")
    print(f"unique member_id    {df['member_id'].nunique():,}")
    print(f"columns             {len(df.columns) - 1}")
    print(f"target base rate    {df[TARGET].mean():.4f}")
    print()
    print("months present:")
    print(sorted(df["month"].unique()))
    print()
    print(f"rows in training window (<= {TRAIN_END}) : "
          f"{(df['month'] <= TRAIN_END).sum():,}")
    print(f"rows in validation window ({VALID_MONTH})  : "
          f"{(df['month'] == VALID_MONTH).sum():,}")
    print(f"rows in test window (>= {TEST_START})     : "
          f"{(df['month'] >= TEST_START).sum():,}")
